Well, I... I mean, I want to be honest with you, but I also don't want it to sound like I'm completely falling apart, you know? I'm trying really hard to keep it together. But... it has been a little tough lately.

I... um... I really hope I don't sound incredibly vain talking about this. I promise I'm usually not a superficial person at all. It's just... like I mentioned before, it's the crookedness. The whole side of my face just looks really swollen and... lopsided. It just looks wrong to me. 

As for whether other people see it... I... I'm not entirely sure? I mean, I want to believe I'm just making a bigger deal out of it than it is, because I know how ridiculous this all sounds. My wife tells me I look fine, and I try so hard to believe her so I don't bother her with it. But when I watch those videos, it just feels so glaringly obvious. I can't help but feel like the students must be staring right at it. Teenagers notice everything, you know? 

But... maybe I am just focusing on it too much and they don't actually see it the way I do? I don't know... do you think I'm just blowing this way out of proportion? I really hope you don't think I'm crazy for obsessing over something like this.

I... well, I mean, I try not to let it bother me as much in person. I really do. When I'm in the classroom, I try to just focus on the kids and the material. But... if I'm being completely honest, it's kind of always there in the back of my mind.

Well, I... I mean, I guess it depends? I really try not to look too closely when I'm just going about my day. I want to be present, you know? I don't want to be that guy who's obsessed with his reflection.

It doesn't really matter if it's a video or a picture or just the bathroom mirror. It's like... it's just my face, so it's always there.

I... well, I do try my best not to let it get to me. I really want to be professional and just do my job. But... yes, I suppose I do have a bit of a routine, if you want to call it that. I hope it doesn't sound too strange. 

When I'm getting ready to post one of those recorded lessons, or before I have to join a virtual parent-teacher conference... I just want to make sure I'm presenting myself well, you know? So I... I might spend a little extra time adjusting the camera angle. Just trying to find a side that doesn't look quite so... off. And I'll replay the clips a few times, just to check. Sometimes I'll catch myself resting my hand on my face again, just to sort of block that side from the camera. 

And... if I'm being completely honest, and I promise I'm working on getting better at this... sometimes I end up re-recording the whole lesson. A few times, actually. It's just... I want it to be perfect for the kids. But I guess I might do it so much that... well, sometimes I miss my other prep work, or I end up running a little late getting things posted for my classes. 

I know it's not ideal, and I really don't want you to think I'm neglecting my students. I care about them so much. I just get so caught up in trying to fix how I look on the screen. Is that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm not coming across as too obsessive.

I really do. When I'm in the classroom, I try to just focus on the kids and the material. But... if I'm being completely honest, it's kind of always there in the back of my mind. \n\nLike, if I'm talking to a student or a parent, I catch myself trying to angle my head a certain way, or maybe I'll rest my hand on my cheek just to sort of... cover it up a little? I hope that doesn't make me sound completely unhinged. I just don't want them to be distracted by it. \n\nAnd photos... oh, I've basically stopped letting anyone take pictures of me. My wife gets a little frustrated because she wants photos of us, and I always offer to be the one taking the picture instead. I tell her I just prefer being behind the camera, which... isn't a total lie, but it's mostly because I can't stomach seeing my face frozen like that. \n\nEven at home, passing a mirror... I try to just glance away quickly so I don't get stuck staring at it and ruin the evening for my wife. I really try to be a good husband and not let this affect our home life. Am I... am I answering your question the right way? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to help me.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror or see a photo of yourself outside of the lesson videos, do you still feel strongly that one side of your face looks noticeably swollen and crooked, or does it seem less obvious to you then?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I mean, I guess it depends? I really try not to look too closely when I'm just going about my day. I want to be present, you know? I don't want to be that guy who's obsessed with his reflection. \n\nBut... yeah, if I do catch a glimpse in the mirror, or if I accidentally see a photo someone took... it still looks just as bad to me. The swelling and the crookedness, it's all right there. It doesn't really matter if it's a video or a picture or just the bathroom mirror. It's like... it's just my face, so it's always there. \n\nI try really hard to just ignore it and not make a fuss, especially around my wife. I don't want to bring her down with my... whatever this is. I just tell myself it's not a big deal and try to move on. Does that... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: When you notice the crookedness or swelling on one side of your face, do you feel a strong urge to do something—like adjusting your posture, covering your face, or mentally going over how you look—to try to fix or neutralize it?\n\nPatient: I... well, I do try my best not to let it get to me. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... well, I know it's probably not the first thing someone notices if they just glance at me in passing. I really try to remind myself of that. But... when I look at those lesson videos, it just seems so glaringly obvious to me. 

I just... I can't stop seeing it. This crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. And once I notice it on the screen, I just get this overwhelming feeling that... well, that everyone else must be seeing it, too. I start worrying that the students, the parents, the other staff... that they're all just staring at my face instead of listening to a single word I'm saying. 

I know that probably sounds a bit silly, and I'm really trying not to let it get to me so much. I want them to focus on the lessons, not on me. But it just feels so... visible, you know? Like it's all anyone can look at. Does that... does that make sense? I hope I don't sound completely irrational. I'm really working on trying to be more objective about it.

Is that... is that an okay thing to talk about here?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how this concern about your facial appearance has been affecting your daily life, especially your ability to teach or interact with students, colleagues, or loved ones?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I mean, I want to be honest with you, but I also don't want it to sound like I'm completely falling apart, you know? I'm trying really hard to keep it together. But... it has been a little tough lately. \n\nI guess I've sort of... fallen behind a bit on things. Like lesson planning, and I usually do a lot of mentoring, plus I coach the basketball team. But I just... I spend so much time worrying about the videos and trying to fix them or hide my face that a lot of my actual work just never gets finished on time anymore. It's not that I don't care about the kids, I really do! It's just... I get so distracted. \n\nAnd, um, the administration has noticed. They've actually started documenting some of my missed deadlines and times I've messed up class coverage. I'm really terrified that I might be close to losing my job over this. Does that... does that make sense? I hope you don't think I'm a terrible teacher for letting this happen.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what you see on your face that concerns you? Do you feel it’s something others can clearly notice, or do you think you might be focusing on something that others may not even see?\n\nPatient: I... um... I really hope I don't sound incredibly vain talking about this. I promise I'm usually not a superficial person at all. It's just... like I mentioned before, it's the crookedness. The whole side of my face just looks really swollen and... lopsided. It just looks wrong to me. \n\nAs for whether other people see it... I... I'm not entirely sure? I mean, I want to believe I'm just making a bigger deal out of it than it is, because I know how ridiculous this all sounds. My wife tells me I look fine, and I try so hard to believe her so I don't bother her with it. But when I watch those videos, it just feels so glaringly obvious. I can't help but feel like the students must be staring right at it. Teenagers notice everything, you know? \n\nBut... maybe I am just focusing on it too much and they don't actually see it the way I do? I don't know... do you think I'm just blowing this way out of proportion? I really hope you don't think I'm crazy for obsessing over something like this.\n\nDoctor: When you're not watching the lesson videos—like during in-person classes, at home, or in photos—does this same concern about the crooked or swollen side of your face bother you as much?\n\nPatient: I... well, I mean, I try not to let it bother me as much in person. I really do. When I'm in the classroom, I try to just focus on the kids and the material. But... if I'm being completely honest, it's kind of always there in the back of my mind. \n\nLike, if I'm talking to a student or a parent, I catch myself trying to angle my head a certain way, or maybe I'll rest my hand on my cheek just to sort of... cover it up a little? I hope that doesn't make me sound completely unhinged. I just don't want them to be distracted by it. \n\nAnd photos... oh, I've basically stopped letting anyone take pictures of me. My wife gets a little frustrated because she wants photos of us, and I always offer to be the one taking the picture instead. I tell her I just prefer being behind the camera, which... isn't a total lie, but it's mostly because I can't stomach seeing my face frozen like that. \n\nEven at home, passing a mirror... I try to just glance away quickly so I don't get stuck staring at it and ruin the evening for my wife. I really try to be a good husband and not let this affect our home life. Am I... am I answering your question the right way? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to help me.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror or see a photo of yourself outside of the lesson videos, do you still feel strongly that one side of your face looks noticeably swollen and crooked, or does it seem less obvious to you then?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I mean, I guess it depends? I really try not to look too closely when I'm just going about my day. I want to be present, you know? I don't want to be that guy who's obsessed with his reflection. \n\nBut... yeah, if I do catch a glimpse in the mirror, or if I accidentally see a photo someone took... it still looks just as bad to me. The swelling and the crookedness, it's all right there. It doesn't really matter if it's a video or a picture or just the bathroom mirror. It's like... it's just my face, so it's always there. \n\nI try really hard to just ignore it and not make a fuss, especially around my wife. I don't want to bring her down with my... whatever this is. I just tell myself it's not a big deal and try to move on. But, um, I guess I'm not doing a very good job of that if I'm sitting here talking to you about it, right? I'm sorry, I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is. Does that... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: When you notice the crookedness or swelling on one side of your face, do you feel a strong urge to do something—like adjusting your posture, covering your face, or mentally going over how you look—to try to fix or neutralize it?\n\nPatient: I... well, I do try my best not to let it get to me. I really want to be professional and just do my job. But... yes, I suppose I do have a bit of a routine, if you want to call it that. I hope it doesn't sound too strange. \n\nWhen I'm getting ready to post one of those recorded lessons, or before I have to join a virtual parent-teacher conference... I just want to make sure I'm presenting myself well, you know? So I... I might spend a little extra time adjusting the camera angle. Just trying to find a side that doesn't look quite so... off. And I'll replay the clips a few times, just to check. Sometimes I'll catch myself resting my hand on my face again, just to sort of block that side from the camera. \n\nAnd... if I'm being completely honest, and I promise I'm working on getting better at this... sometimes I end up re-recording the whole lesson. A few times, actually. It's just... I want it to be perfect for the kids. But I guess I might do it so much that... well, sometimes I miss my other prep work, or I end up running a little late getting things posted for my classes. \n\nI know it's not ideal, and I really don't want you to think I'm neglecting my students. I care about them so much. I just get so caught up in trying to fix how I look on the screen. Is that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm not coming across as too obsessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concern about your facial appearance has caused you significant distress or has it affected your ability to function normally in your work or personal life?\n\nPatient: I mean... I try so hard not to let it affect my work. I really, really do. I care about my students and the school so much, and I want to be a good teacher for them. But... well, I guess if I'm being completely honest, and I know this probably sounds terrible... it has been getting a bit difficult lately. \n\nI just... I spend so much time worrying about the videos and trying to fix how I look, that sometimes other things sort of... slip through the cracks? Like, I've fallen a little behind on my regular lesson planning. And I help mentor some of the newer teachers, and I coach the basketball team, but lately, I just... I haven't been able to keep up with all of it because my main work just isn't getting finished on time anymore. \n\nIt's actually gotten to the point where... well, the administration has noticed. They've started documenting some missed deadlines and times when I couldn't cover things I was supposed to. I'm just so terrified that I might be close to losing my position. Which would be awful, because I love teaching. I really do. I just... I feel terrible that I'm letting everyone down. Does that make sense? I hope you don't think I'm a bad teacher... I'm really trying my best to fix this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts—like checking your appearance, adjusting your posture, or going over how you look in your mind—because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: I... um, I think we might have touched on this just a little bit earlier, but... yes, I suppose I do. I... um, I haven't really kept track of the exact hours or anything like that. I try really hard to stay focused on my lesson plans and grading when I'm not on camera. I really do.

I really hope I don't sound incredibly vain talking about this. I promise I'm usually not a superficial person at all. It's just... like I mentioned before, it's the crookedness. The whole side of my face just looks really swollen and... lopsided. It just looks wrong to me. \n\nAs for whether other people see it... I... I'm not entirely sure? I mean, I want to believe I'm just making a bigger deal out of it than it is, because I know how ridiculous this all sounds. My wife tells me I look fine, and I try so hard to believe her so I don't bother her with it. But when I watch those videos, it just feels so glaringly obvious. I can't help but feel like the students must be staring right at it. Teenagers notice everything, you know? \n\nBut... maybe I am just focusing on it too much and they don't actually see it the way I do? I don't know... do you think I'm just blowing this way out of proportion? I really hope you don't think I'm crazy for obsessing over something like this.\n\nDoctor: When you're not watching the lesson videos—like during in-person classes, at home, or in photos—does this same concern about the crooked or swollen side of your face bother you as much?\n\nPatient: I... well, I mean, I try not to let it bother me as much in person. I really do. When I'm in the classroom, I try to just focus on the kids and the material. But... if I'm being completely honest, it's kind of always there in the back of my mind. \n\nLike, if I'm talking to a student or a parent, I catch myself trying to angle my head a certain way, or maybe I'll rest my hand on my cheek just to sort of... cover it up a little? I hope that doesn't make me sound completely unhinged. I just don't want them to be distracted by it. \n\nAnd photos... oh, I've basically stopped letting anyone take pictures of me. My wife gets a little frustrated because she wants photos of us, and I always offer to be the one taking the picture instead. I tell her I just prefer being behind the camera, which... isn't a total lie, but it's mostly because I can't stomach seeing my face frozen like that. \n\nEven at home, passing a mirror... I try to just glance away quickly so I don't get stuck staring at it and ruin the evening for my wife. I really try to be a good husband and not let this affect our home life. Am I... am I answering your question the right way? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to help me.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror or see a photo of yourself outside of the lesson videos, do you still feel strongly that one side of your face looks noticeably swollen and crooked, or does it seem less obvious to you then?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I mean, I guess it depends? I really try not to look too closely when I'm just going about my day. I want to be present, you know? I don't want to be that guy who's obsessed with his reflection. \n\nBut... yeah, if I do catch a glimpse in the mirror, or if I accidentally see a photo someone took... it still looks just as bad to me. The swelling and the crookedness, it's all right there. It doesn't really matter if it's a video or a picture or just the bathroom mirror. It's like... it's just my face, so it's always there. \n\nI try really hard to just ignore it and not make a fuss, especially around my wife. I don't want to bring her down with my... whatever this is. I just tell myself it's not a big deal and try to move on. But, um, I guess I'm not doing a very good job of that if I'm sitting here talking to you about it, right? I'm sorry, I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is. Does that... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: When you notice the crookedness or swelling on one side of your face, do you feel a strong urge to do something—like adjusting your posture, covering your face, or mentally going over how you look—to try to fix or neutralize it?\n\nPatient: I... well, I do try my best not to let it get to me. I really want to be professional and just do my job. But... yes, I suppose I do have a bit of a routine, if you want to call it that. I hope it doesn't sound too strange. \n\nWhen I'm getting ready to post one of those recorded lessons, or before I have to join a virtual parent-teacher conference... I just want to make sure I'm presenting myself well, you know? So I... I might spend a little extra time adjusting the camera angle. Just trying to find a side that doesn't look quite so... off. And I'll replay the clips a few times, just to check. Sometimes I'll catch myself resting my hand on my face again, just to sort of block that side from the camera. \n\nAnd... if I'm being completely honest, and I promise I'm working on getting better at this... sometimes I end up re-recording the whole lesson. A few times, actually. It's just... I want it to be perfect for the kids. But I guess I might do it so much that... well, sometimes I miss my other prep work, or I end up running a little late getting things posted for my classes. \n\nI know it's not ideal, and I really don't want you to think I'm neglecting my students. I care about them so much. I just get so caught up in trying to fix how I look on the screen. Is that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm not coming across as too obsessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concern about your facial appearance has caused you significant distress or has it affected your ability to function normally in your work or personal life?\n\nPatient: I mean... I try so hard not to let it affect my work. I really, really do. I care about my students and the school so much, and I want to be a good teacher for them. But... well, I guess if I'm being completely honest, and I know this probably sounds terrible... it has been getting a bit difficult lately. \n\nI just... I spend so much time worrying about the videos and trying to fix how I look, that sometimes other things sort of... slip through the cracks? Like, I've fallen a little behind on my regular lesson planning. And I help mentor some of the newer teachers, and I coach the basketball team, but lately, I just... I haven't been able to keep up with all of it because my main work just isn't getting finished on time anymore. \n\nIt's actually gotten to the point where... well, the administration has noticed. They've started documenting some missed deadlines and times when I couldn't cover things I was supposed to. I'm just so terrified that I might be close to losing my position. Which would be awful, because I love teaching. I really do. I just... I feel terrible that I'm letting everyone down. Does that make sense? I hope you don't think I'm a bad teacher... I'm really trying my best to fix this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts—like checking your appearance, adjusting your posture, or going over how you look in your mind—because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: I... um, I think we might have touched on this just a little bit earlier, but... yes, I suppose I do. I really try not to make a big deal out of it, and I hope it doesn't sound like I'm completely obsessed or anything. I just want to put my best foot forward for the school and the parents.\n\nBut, well, before I post a recorded lesson or join a virtual parent conference, I do find myself... repeating a few things. Like, I'll keep changing the camera angle, trying to find a spot where my face doesn't look so... uneven. And I'll replay the clips over and over, just to check. I also catch myself resting my hand on my face a lot, just to sort of hide that side. \n\nAnd, like I mentioned before, sometimes I end up re-recording the whole lesson. A few times, actually. It's just... I keep thinking if I try one more time, maybe it won't look so bad. But then I end up doing it so many times that I miss my prep work and wind up late for my classes. \n\nI know it's not the best habit, and I'm really trying to get better at just letting it go. I promise I'm not usually this... difficult about things. I just want everything to be right for the kids. Does that... does that answer your question? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if the crookedness or swelling you see on your face is something that you believe is clearly visible to others, or if it's more of a feeling you have about your appearance that may not be obvious to people around you?\n\nPatient: I... well, I know it's probably not the first thing someone notices if they just glance at me in passing. I really try to remind myself of that. But... when I look at those lesson videos, it just seems so glaringly obvious to me. \n\nI just... I can't stop seeing it. This crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. And once I notice it on the screen, I just get this overwhelming feeling that... well, that everyone else must be seeing it, too. I start worrying that the students, the parents, the other staff... that they're all just staring at my face instead of listening to a single word I'm saying. \n\nI know that probably sounds a bit silly, and I'm really trying not to let it get to me so much. I want them to focus on the lessons, not on me. But it just feels so... visible, you know? Like it's all anyone can look at. Does that... does that make sense? I hope I don't sound completely irrational. I'm really working on trying to be more objective about it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are times when you feel completely consumed by the idea that something is wrong with your face, even when you know logically that it might not be as noticeable as it feels in the moment?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I guess there are times like that. I really try to be logical about it, I promise I do. I tell myself that people are busy and they have better things to do than scrutinize my face. But... well, I guess the reason I'm even here talking to you today is because... I just can't stop seeing it. \n\nWhen I look at those videos, that crooked, swollen side of my face is just... it's all I can focus on. And even though I try to tell myself it's not that bad, I get this awful, consuming feeling that everyone else—the students, the parents, the other teachers—that they're all just staring at it. Like they can't even hear what I'm teaching because my face is so distracting. \n\nI know I shouldn't let it take over my thoughts like that. I really want to be professional and just focus on the curriculum. But it just takes over sometimes, no matter how hard I try to push it away. I hope that doesn't make me sound too... unstable or anything. I'm really trying my best to handle it better. Am I making sense?\n\nDoctor: How much time do you typically spend each day thinking about how your face looks, especially when you're not recording lesson videos or actively checking your appearance?\n\nPatient: I... um, I haven't really kept track of the exact hours or anything like that. I really do. \n\nBut, I suppose... even when I'm just sitting at my desk or making dinner, it kind of lingers in the back of my mind. Like, I'll be reading a student's essay and suddenly I'm wondering if they were looking at my face when I assigned it. Or I'll just be walking through the house and I catch myself thinking about how I need to fix the lighting for the next video so it doesn't look so... so bad. \n\nI'd say it's... well, it's on my mind a fair amount throughout the day. Not every single second, of course! I'm still getting my work done. But it pops up a lot more than I'd like to admit. I really hope that doesn't make me sound too distracted from my job. I'm trying to be better about leaving work at work, so to speak. I... well, I wouldn't say I do things over and over in a way that's... out of control, or anything. I just want to make sure I'm presenting myself well for the students, you know? It's important to be professional. 

But, um... I guess before I post a recorded lesson, or if I have to join a virtual parent conference, I do... well, I might adjust the camera angle a few times. Just to make sure the lighting isn't emphasizing... the crookedness. And I'll replay the clips a bit to check. Sometimes, I catch myself sort of resting my head on my hand, just to cover that side of my face so it's not so obvious.

But I just... I spend so much time worrying about the videos and trying to fix them or hide my face that a lot of my actual work just never gets finished on time anymore. It's not that I don't care about the kids, I really do! It's just... I get so distracted. \n\nAnd, um, the administration has noticed. They've actually started documenting some of my missed deadlines and times I've messed up class coverage. I'm really terrified that I might be close to losing my job over this. Does that... does that make sense? I hope you don't think I'm a terrible teacher for letting this happen.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what you see on your face that concerns you? Do you feel it’s something others can clearly notice, or do you think you might be focusing on something that others may not even see?\n\nPatient: I... um... I really hope I don't sound incredibly vain talking about this. I promise I'm usually not a superficial person at all. It's just... like I mentioned before, it's the crookedness. The whole side of my face just looks really swollen and... lopsided. It just looks wrong to me. \n\nAs for whether other people see it... I... I'm not entirely sure? I mean, I want to believe I'm just making a bigger deal out of it than it is, because I know how ridiculous this all sounds. My wife tells me I look fine, and I try so hard to believe her so I don't bother her with it. But when I watch those videos, it just feels so glaringly obvious. I can't help but feel like the students must be staring right at it. Teenagers notice everything, you know? \n\nBut... maybe I am just focusing on it too much and they don't actually see it the way I do? I don't know... do you think I'm just blowing this way out of proportion? I really hope you don't think I'm crazy for obsessing over something like this.\n\nDoctor: When you're not watching the lesson videos—like during in-person classes, at home, or in photos—does this same concern about the crooked or swollen side of your face bother you as much?\n\nPatient: I... well, I mean, I try not to let it bother me as much in person. I really do. When I'm in the classroom, I try to just focus on the kids and the material. But... if I'm being completely honest, it's kind of always there in the back of my mind. \n\nLike, if I'm talking to a student or a parent, I catch myself trying to angle my head a certain way, or maybe I'll rest my hand on my cheek just to sort of... cover it up a little? I hope that doesn't make me sound completely unhinged. I just don't want them to be distracted by it. \n\nAnd photos... oh, I've basically stopped letting anyone take pictures of me. My wife gets a little frustrated because she wants photos of us, and I always offer to be the one taking the picture instead. I tell her I just prefer being behind the camera, which... isn't a total lie, but it's mostly because I can't stomach seeing my face frozen like that. \n\nEven at home, passing a mirror... I try to just glance away quickly so I don't get stuck staring at it and ruin the evening for my wife. I really try to be a good husband and not let this affect our home life. Am I... am I answering your question the right way? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to help me.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror or see a photo of yourself outside of the lesson videos, do you still feel strongly that one side of your face looks noticeably swollen and crooked, or does it seem less obvious to you then?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I mean, I guess it depends? I really try not to look too closely when I'm just going about my day. I want to be present, you know? I don't want to be that guy who's obsessed with his reflection. \n\nBut... yeah, if I do catch a glimpse in the mirror, or if I accidentally see a photo someone took... it still looks just as bad to me. The swelling and the crookedness, it's all right there. It doesn't really matter if it's a video or a picture or just the bathroom mirror. It's like... it's just my face, so it's always there. \n\nI try really hard to just ignore it and not make a fuss, especially around my wife. I don't want to bring her down with my... whatever this is. I just tell myself it's not a big deal and try to move on. But, um, I guess I'm not doing a very good job of that if I'm sitting here talking to you about it, right? I'm sorry, I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is. Does that... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: When you notice the crookedness or swelling on one side of your face, do you feel a strong urge to do something—like adjusting your posture, covering your face, or mentally going over how you look—to try to fix or neutralize it?\n\nPatient: I... well, I do try my best not to let it get to me. I really want to be professional and just do my job. But... yes, I suppose I do have a bit of a routine, if you want to call it that. I hope it doesn't sound too strange. \n\nWhen I'm getting ready to post one of those recorded lessons, or before I have to join a virtual parent-teacher conference... I just want to make sure I'm presenting myself well, you know? So I... I might spend a little extra time adjusting the camera angle. Just trying to find a side that doesn't look quite so... off. And I'll replay the clips a few times, just to check. Sometimes I'll catch myself resting my hand on my face again, just to sort of block that side from the camera. \n\nAnd... if I'm being completely honest, and I promise I'm working on getting better at this... sometimes I end up re-recording the whole lesson. I care about them so much. I just get so caught up in trying to fix how I look on the screen. Is that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm not coming across as too obsessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concern about your facial appearance has caused you significant distress or has it affected your ability to function normally in your work or personal life?\n\nPatient: I mean... I try so hard not to let it affect my work. I really, really do. I care about my students and the school so much, and I want to be a good teacher for them. But... well, I guess if I'm being completely honest, and I know this probably sounds terrible... it has been getting a bit difficult lately. \n\nI just... I spend so much time worrying about the videos and trying to fix how I look, that sometimes other things sort of... slip through the cracks? Like, I've fallen a little behind on my regular lesson planning. And I help mentor some of the newer teachers, and I coach the basketball team, but lately, I just... I haven't been able to keep up with all of it because my main work just isn't getting finished on time anymore. \n\nIt's actually gotten to the point where... well, the administration has noticed. I'm really trying my best to fix this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts—like checking your appearance, adjusting your posture, or going over how you look in your mind—because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: I... um, I think we might have touched on this just a little bit earlier, but... yes, I suppose I do. I really try not to make a big deal out of it, and I hope it doesn't sound like I'm completely obsessed or anything. I just want to put my best foot forward for the school and the parents.\n\nBut, well, before I post a recorded lesson or join a virtual parent conference, I do find myself... repeating a few things. Like, I'll keep changing the camera angle, trying to find a spot where my face doesn't look so... uneven. And I'll replay the clips over and over, just to check. I also catch myself resting my hand on my face a lot, just to sort of hide that side. \n\nAnd, like I mentioned before, sometimes I end up re-recording the whole lesson. A few times, actually. It's just... I keep thinking if I try one more time, maybe it won't look so bad. But then I end up doing it so many times that I miss my prep work and wind up late for my classes. \n\nI know it's not the best habit, and I'm really trying to get better at just letting it go. I promise I'm not usually this... difficult about things. I just want everything to be right for the kids. Does that... does that answer your question? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if the crookedness or swelling you see on your face is something that you believe is clearly visible to others, or if it's more of a feeling you have about your appearance that may not be obvious to people around you?\n\nPatient: I... well, I know it's probably not the first thing someone notices if they just glance at me in passing. I really try to remind myself of that. But... when I look at those lesson videos, it just seems so glaringly obvious to me. \n\nI just... I can't stop seeing it. I can't stop seeing this crooked, kind of swollen-looking side of my face. And once I notice it, it completely derails me. I just get this overwhelming feeling that the students, their parents, the other staff... they must all be staring right at my face instead of actually listening to a single word I’m teaching. Is that... is that an okay thing to talk about here?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how this concern about your facial appearance has been affecting your daily life, especially your ability to teach or interact with students, colleagues, or loved ones?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I mean, I want to be honest with you, but I also don't want it to sound like I'm completely falling apart, you know? I'm trying really hard to keep it together. But... it has been a little tough lately. \n\nI guess I've sort of... fallen behind a bit on things. Like lesson planning, and I usually do a lot of mentoring, plus I coach the basketball team. But I just... I spend so much time worrying about the videos and trying to fix them or hide my face that a lot of my actual work just never gets finished on time anymore. It's not that I don't care about the kids, I really do! It's just... I get so distracted. \n\nAnd, um, the administration has noticed. They've actually started documenting some of my missed deadlines and times I've messed up class coverage. I'm really terrified that I might be close to losing my job over this. Does that... does that make sense? I hope you don't think I'm a terrible teacher for letting this happen.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what you see on your face that concerns you? Do you feel it’s something others can clearly notice, or do you think you might be focusing on something that others may not even see?\n\nPatient: I... um... I really hope I don't sound incredibly vain talking about this. I promise I'm usually not a superficial person at all. It's just... like I mentioned before, it's the crookedness. The whole side of my face just looks really swollen and... lopsided. It just looks wrong to me. \n\nAs for whether other people see it... I... I'm not entirely sure? I mean, I want to believe I'm just making a bigger deal out of it than it is, because I know how ridiculous this all sounds. My wife tells me I look fine, and I try so hard to believe her so I don't bother her with it. But when I watch those videos, it just feels so glaringly obvious. I can't help but feel like the students must be staring right at it. Teenagers notice everything, you know? \n\nBut... maybe I am just focusing on it too much and they don't actually see it the way I do? I don't know... do you think I'm just blowing this way out of proportion? I really hope you don't think I'm crazy for obsessing over something like this.\n\nDoctor: When you're not watching the lesson videos—like during in-person classes, at home, or in photos—does this same concern about the crooked or swollen side of your face bother you as much?\n\nPatient: I... well, I mean, I try not to let it bother me as much in person. I really do. When I'm in the classroom, I try to just focus on the kids and the material. But... if I'm being completely honest, it's kind of always there in the back of my mind. \n\nLike, if I'm talking to a student or a parent, I catch myself trying to angle my head a certain way, or maybe I'll rest my hand on my cheek just to sort of... cover it up a little? I hope that doesn't make me sound completely unhinged. I just don't want them to be distracted by it. \n\nAnd photos... oh, I've basically stopped letting anyone take pictures of me. My wife gets a little frustrated because she wants photos of us, and I always offer to be the one taking the picture instead. I tell her I just prefer being behind the camera, which... isn't a total lie, but it's mostly because I can't stomach seeing my face frozen like that. \n\nEven at home, passing a mirror... I try to just glance away quickly so I don't get stuck staring at it and ruin the evening for my wife. I really try to be a good husband and not let this affect our home life. Am I... am I answering your question the right way? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to help me.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror or see a photo of yourself outside of the lesson videos, do you still feel strongly that one side of your face looks noticeably swollen and crooked, or does it seem less obvious to you then?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I mean, I guess it depends? I really try not to look too closely when I'm just going about my day. I want to be present, you know? I don't want to be that guy who's obsessed with his reflection. \n\nBut... yeah, if I do catch a glimpse in the mirror, or if I accidentally see a photo someone took... it still looks just as bad to me. The swelling and the crookedness, it's all right there. It doesn't really matter if it's a video or a picture or just the bathroom mirror. It's like... it's just my face, so it's always there. \n\nI try really hard to just ignore it and not make a fuss, especially around my wife. I don't want to bring her down with my... whatever this is. I just tell myself it's not a big deal and try to move on. But, um, I guess I'm not doing a very good job of that if I'm sitting here talking to you about it, right? I'm sorry, I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is. Does that... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: When you notice the crookedness or swelling on one side of your face, do you feel a strong urge to do something—like adjusting your posture, covering your face, or mentally going over how you look—to try to fix or neutralize it?\n\nPatient: I... well, I do try my best not to let it get to me. I really want to be professional and just do my job. But... yes, I suppose I do have a bit of a routine, if you want to call it that. I hope it doesn't sound too strange. \n\nWhen I'm getting ready to post one of those recorded lessons, or before I have to join a virtual parent-teacher conference... I just want to make sure I'm presenting myself well, you know? So I... I might spend a little extra time adjusting the camera angle. Just trying to find a side that doesn't look quite so... off. And I'll replay the clips a few times, just to check. Sometimes I'll catch myself resting my hand on my face again, just to sort of block that side from the camera. \n\nAnd... if I'm being completely honest, and I promise I'm working on getting better at this... sometimes I end up re-recording the whole lesson. A few times, actually. It's just... I want it to be perfect for the kids. But I guess I might do it so much that... well, sometimes I miss my other prep work, or I end up running a little late getting things posted for my classes. \n\nI know it's not ideal, and I really don't want you to think I'm neglecting my students. I care about them so much. I just get so caught up in trying to fix how I look on the screen. Is that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm not coming across as too obsessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concern about your facial appearance has caused you significant distress or has it affected your ability to function normally in your work or personal life?\n\nPatient: I mean... I try so hard not to let it affect my work. I really, really do. I care about my students and the school so much, and I want to be a good teacher for them. But... well, I guess if I'm being completely honest, and I know this probably sounds terrible... it has been getting a bit difficult lately. \n\nI just... I spend so much time worrying about the videos and trying to fix how I look, that sometimes other things sort of... slip through the cracks? Like, I've fallen a little behind on my regular lesson planning. And I help mentor some of the newer teachers, and I coach the basketball team, but lately, I just... I haven't been able to keep up with all of it because my main work just isn't getting finished on time anymore. \n\nIt's actually gotten to the point where... well, the administration has noticed. They've started documenting some missed deadlines and times when I couldn't cover things I was supposed to. I'm just so terrified that I might be close to losing my position. Which would be awful, because I love teaching. I really do. I just... I feel terrible that I'm letting everyone down. Does that make sense? I hope you don't think I'm a bad teacher... I'm really trying my best to fix this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts—like checking your appearance, adjusting your posture, or going over how you look in your mind—because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: I... um, I think we might have touched on this just a little bit earlier, but... yes, I suppose I do. I really try not to make a big deal out of it, and I hope it doesn't sound like I'm completely obsessed or anything. I just want to put my best foot forward for the school and the parents.\n\nBut, well, before I post a recorded lesson or join a virtual parent conference, I do find myself... repeating a few things. Like, I'll keep changing the camera angle, trying to find a spot where my face doesn't look so... uneven. And I'll replay the clips over and over, just to check. I also catch myself resting my hand on my face a lot, just to sort of hide that side. \n\nAnd, like I mentioned before, sometimes I end up re-recording the whole lesson. A few times, actually. It's just... I keep thinking if I try one more time, maybe it won't look so bad. But then I end up doing it so many times that I miss my prep work and wind up late for my classes. \n\nI know it's not the best habit, and I'm really trying to get better at just letting it go. I promise I'm not usually this... difficult about things. I just want everything to be right for the kids. Does that... does that answer your question? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of information.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if the crookedness or swelling you see on your face is something that you believe is clearly visible to others, or if it's more of a feeling you have about your appearance that may not be obvious to people around you?\n\nPatient: I... well, I know it's probably not the first thing someone notices if they just glance at me in passing. I really try to remind myself of that. But... when I look at those lesson videos, it just seems so glaringly obvious to me. \n\nI just... I can't stop seeing it. This crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. And once I notice it on the screen, I just get this overwhelming feeling that... well, that everyone else must be seeing it, too. I start worrying that the students, the parents, the other staff... that they're all just staring at my face instead of listening to a single word I'm saying. \n\nI know that probably sounds a bit silly, and I'm really trying not to let it get to me so much. I want them to focus on the lessons, not on me. But it just feels so... visible, you know? Like it's all anyone can look at. Does that... does that make sense? I hope I don't sound completely irrational. I'm really working on trying to be more objective about it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are times when you feel completely consumed by the idea that something is wrong with your face, even when you know logically that it might not be as noticeable as it feels in the moment?\n\nPatient: I... yeah, I guess there are times like that. I really try to be logical about it, I promise I do. I tell myself that people are busy and they have better things to do than scrutinize my face. But... well, I guess the reason I'm even here talking to you today is because... I just can't stop seeing it. \n\nWhen I look at those videos, that crooked, swollen side of my face is just... it's all I can focus on. And even though I try to tell myself it's not that bad, I get this awful, consuming feeling that everyone else—the students, the parents, the other teachers—that they're all just staring at it. Like they can't even hear what I'm teaching because my face is so distracting. \n\nI know I shouldn't let it take over my thoughts like that. I really want to be professional and just focus on the curriculum. But it just takes over sometimes, no matter how hard I try to push it away. I hope that doesn't make me sound too... unstable or anything. I'm really trying my best to handle it better. Am I making sense?\n\nDoctor: How much time do you typically spend each day thinking about how your face looks, especially when you're not recording lesson videos or actively checking your appearance?\n\nPatient: I... um, I haven't really kept track of the exact hours or anything like that. I try really hard to stay focused on my lesson plans and grading when I'm not on camera. I really do. \n\nBut, I suppose... even when I'm just sitting at my desk or making dinner, it kind of lingers in the back of my mind. Like, I'll be reading a student's essay and suddenly I'm wondering if they were looking at my face when I assigned it. Or I'll just be walking through the house and I catch myself thinking about how I need to fix the lighting for the next video so it doesn't look so... so bad. \n\nI'd say it's... well, it's on my mind a fair amount throughout the day. Not every single second, of course! I'm still getting my work done. But it pops up a lot more than I'd like to admit. I really hope that doesn't make me sound too distracted from my job. I'm trying to be better about leaving work at work, so to speak. Is that... is that the kind of estimate you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: When you’re concerned about how your face looks, do you find yourself doing certain things over and over—like checking your reflection, adjusting your posture, or replaying how you looked in your mind—to try to reduce the worry or make it go away?\n\nPatient: I... well, I wouldn't say I do things over and over in a way that's... out of control, or anything. I just want to make sure I'm presenting myself well for the students, you know? It's important to be professional. \n\nBut, um... I guess before I post a recorded lesson, or if I have to join a virtual parent conference, I do... well, I might adjust the camera angle a few times. Just to make sure the lighting isn't emphasizing... the crookedness. And I'll replay the clips a bit to check. Sometimes, I catch myself sort of resting my head on my hand, just to cover that side of my face so it's not so obvious. \n\nI mean, I just want the videos to be good. But I suppose there have been a few times where I've... re-recorded the whole lesson a couple of times because I just couldn't stand how it looked. And, well, I might have gotten a little behind on my prep work because of it, or been a tiny bit late getting the class materials up. But I always get it done! I don't want you to think I'm neglecting my students. I just... I want it to be right. Does that sound like what you were asking about? I hope that doesn't seem too unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about how your face looks, does it cause you a lot of anxiety or emotional pain, even when you know others might not notice it?\n\nPatient: I... well, \"pain\" might be a bit of a strong word. I really don't want to sound like I'm... falling apart over it or anything. I have to keep it together for the high schoolers, you know? They need a teacher who is present and capable, not someone who's a mess. \n\nIt does make me... a little anxious, I suppose. Just that sort of tight, uncomfortable feeling when you're worried you aren't putting your best foot forward. But I wouldn't say it's unbearable emotional pain. I really try to just take a deep breath, put on a brave face, and push through it. I know everyone gets insecure sometimes, so I try to just remind myself of that. \n\nI hope I'm answering this right... I just don't want to make it sound like I'm completely overwhelmed when I'm really trying my best to handle it like a professional. Does that make sense? I hope that's an okay answer.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

But... well, I guess the reason I'm even here talking to you today is because... I just can't stop seeing it. \n\nWhen I look at those videos, that crooked, swollen side of my face is just... it's all I can focus on. And even though I try to tell myself it's not that bad, I get this awful, consuming feeling that everyone else—the students, the parents, the other teachers—that they're all just staring at it. Like they can't even hear what I'm teaching because my face is so distracting. \n\nI know I shouldn't let it take over my thoughts like that. I really want to be professional and just focus on the curriculum. But it just takes over sometimes, no matter how hard I try to push it away. I hope that doesn't make me sound too... unstable or anything. I'm really trying my best to handle it better. Am I making sense?\n\nDoctor: How much time do you typically spend each day thinking about how your face looks, especially when you're not recording lesson videos or actively checking your appearance?\n\nPatient: I... um, I haven't really kept track of the exact hours or anything like that. I try really hard to stay focused on my lesson plans and grading when I'm not on camera. I really do. \n\nBut, I suppose... even when I'm just sitting at my desk or making dinner, it kind of lingers in the back of my mind. Like, I'll be reading a student's essay and suddenly I'm wondering if they were looking at my face when I assigned it. Or I'll just be walking through the house and I catch myself thinking about how I need to fix the lighting for the next video so it doesn't look so... so bad. \n\nI'd say it's... well, it's on my mind a fair amount throughout the day. Not every single second, of course! I'm still getting my work done. But it pops up a lot more than I'd like to admit. I really hope that doesn't make me sound too distracted from my job. I'm trying to be better about leaving work at work, so to speak. Is that... is that the kind of estimate you were looking for?\n\nDoctor: When you’re concerned about how your face looks, do you find yourself doing certain things over and over—like checking your reflection, adjusting your posture, or replaying how you looked in your mind—to try to reduce the worry or make it go away?\n\nPatient: I... well, I wouldn't say I do things over and over in a way that's... out of control, or anything. I just want to make sure I'm presenting myself well for the students, you know? It's important to be professional. \n\nBut, um... I guess before I post a recorded lesson, or if I have to join a virtual parent conference, I do... well, I might adjust the camera angle a few times. Just to make sure the lighting isn't emphasizing... the crookedness. And I'll replay the clips a bit to check. Sometimes, I catch myself sort of resting my head on my hand, just to cover that side of my face so it's not so obvious. \n\nI mean, I just want the videos to be good. But I suppose there have been a few times where I've... re-recorded the whole lesson a couple of times because I just couldn't stand how it looked. And, well, I might have gotten a little behind on my prep work because of it, or been a tiny bit late getting the class materials up. But I always get it done! I don't want you to think I'm neglecting my students. I just... I want it to be right. Does that sound like what you were asking about? I hope that doesn't seem too unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about how your face looks, does it cause you a lot of anxiety or emotional pain, even when you know others might not notice it?\n\nPatient: I... well, \"pain\" might be a bit of a strong word. I really don't want to sound like I'm... falling apart over it or anything. I have to keep it together for the high schoolers, you know? They need a teacher who is present and capable, not someone who's a mess. \n\nIt does make me... a little anxious, I suppose. Just that sort of tight, uncomfortable feeling when you're worried you aren't putting your best foot forward. But I wouldn't say it's unbearable emotional pain. I really try to just take a deep breath, put on a brave face, and push through it. I know everyone gets insecure sometimes, so I try to just remind myself of that. \n\nI hope I'm answering this right... I just don't want to make it sound like I'm completely overwhelmed when I'm really trying my best to handle it like a professional. Does that make sense? I hope that's an okay answer.\n\nDoctor: When you think about how your face looks, especially the crookedness or swelling you’ve described, do you ever feel intense emotional pain, panic, or overwhelming anxiety—even if only for a short time?\n\nPatient: I... um... panic is a really strong word. I don't want you to think I'm having, like, full-blown meltdowns in the middle of the school day or anything. I really do try to keep things in perspective. \n\nBut... well, I guess there are moments, usually when I'm alone, where it hits me a bit harder. Like if I catch a really bad angle in the mirror right before I have to record, or if I'm editing and I just... I can't stop staring at how uneven my jaw looks. My chest might get a little tight, and I'll feel this rush of... I guess you could call it anxiety. It just feels really heavy for a minute, like I just want to hide my face from everyone so they don't have to look at it. \n\nBut it passes! I always manage to pull myself together and get the videos done. I just take a moment, tell myself it's fine, and move on. I really don't let it stop me from doing my job. I'm sorry, I know you asked this already, I just... I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information without making it sound like I'm completely falling apart. Is it normal to feel a little overwhelmed like that sometimes?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Am I making sense so far? \n\nIt's just... I'm here because I've been really struggling with these lesson videos I have to record for my classes. Whenever I watch them back, I just... I can't stop seeing this crooked, kind of swollen-looking side of my face. And once I notice it, it completely derails me. I just get this overwhelming feeling that the students, their parents, the other staff... they must all be staring right at my face instead of actually listening to a single word I’m teaching. Is that... is that an okay thing to talk about here?\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me how this concern about your facial appearance has been affecting your daily life, especially your ability to teach or interact with students, colleagues, or loved ones?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I mean, I want to be honest with you, but I also don't want it to sound like I'm completely falling apart, you know? I'm trying really hard to keep it together. But... it has been a little tough lately. \n\nI guess I've sort of... fallen behind a bit on things. Like lesson planning, and I usually do a lot of mentoring, plus I coach the basketball team. But I just... I spend so much time worrying about the videos and trying to fix them or hide my face that a lot of my actual work just never gets finished on time anymore. It's not that I don't care about the kids, I really do! It's just... I get so distracted. \n\nAnd, um, the administration has noticed. They've actually started documenting some of my missed deadlines and times I've messed up class coverage. I'm really terrified that I might be close to losing my job over this. Does that... does that make sense? I hope you don't think I'm a terrible teacher for letting this happen.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what you see on your face that concerns you? Do you feel it’s something others can clearly notice, or do you think you might be focusing on something that others may not even see?\n\nPatient: I... um... I really hope I don't sound incredibly vain talking about this. I promise I'm usually not a superficial person at all. It's just... like I mentioned before, it's the crookedness. The whole side of my face just looks really swollen and... lopsided. It just looks wrong to me. \n\nAs for whether other people see it... I... I'm not entirely sure? I mean, I want to believe I'm just making a bigger deal out of it than it is, because I know how ridiculous this all sounds. My wife tells me I look fine, and I try so hard to believe her so I don't bother her with it. But when I watch those videos, it just feels so glaringly obvious. I can't help but feel like the students must be staring right at it. Teenagers notice everything, you know? \n\nBut... maybe I am just focusing on it too much and they don't actually see it the way I do? I don't know... do you think I'm just blowing this way out of proportion? I really hope you don't think I'm crazy for obsessing over something like this.\n\nDoctor: When you're not watching the lesson videos—like during in-person classes, at home, or in photos—does this same concern about the crooked or swollen side of your face bother you as much?\n\nPatient: I... well, I mean, I try not to let it bother me as much in person. I really do. When I'm in the classroom, I try to just focus on the kids and the material. But... if I'm being completely honest, it's kind of always there in the back of my mind. \n\nLike, if I'm talking to a student or a parent, I catch myself trying to angle my head a certain way, or maybe I'll rest my hand on my cheek just to sort of... cover it up a little? I hope that doesn't make me sound completely unhinged. I just don't want them to be distracted by it. \n\nAnd photos... oh, I've basically stopped letting anyone take pictures of me. My wife gets a little frustrated because she wants photos of us, and I always offer to be the one taking the picture instead. I tell her I just prefer being behind the camera, which... isn't a total lie, but it's mostly because I can't stomach seeing my face frozen like that. \n\nEven at home, passing a mirror... I try to just glance away quickly so I don't get stuck staring at it and ruin the evening for my wife. I really try to be a good husband and not let this affect our home life. Am I... am I answering your question the right way? I just want to make sure I'm giving you what you need to help me.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror or see a photo of yourself outside of the lesson videos, do you still feel strongly that one side of your face looks noticeably swollen and crooked, or does it seem less obvious to you then?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I mean, I guess it depends? I really try not to look too closely when I'm just going about my day. I want to be present, you know? I don't want to be that guy who's obsessed with his reflection. \n\nBut... yeah, if I do catch a glimpse in the mirror, or if I accidentally see a photo someone took... it still looks just as bad to me. The swelling and the crookedness, it's all right there. It doesn't really matter if it's a video or a picture or just the bathroom mirror. It's like... it's just my face, so it's always there. \n\nI try really hard to just ignore it and not make a fuss, especially around my wife. I don't want to bring her down with my... whatever this is. I just tell myself it's not a big deal and try to move on. But, um, I guess I'm not doing a very good job of that if I'm sitting here talking to you about it, right? I'm sorry, I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is. Does that... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: When you notice the crookedness or swelling on one side of your face, do you feel a strong urge to do something—like adjusting your posture, covering your face, or mentally going over how you look—to try to fix or neutralize it?\n\nPatient: I... well, I do try my best not to let it get to me. I really want to be professional and just do my job. But... yes, I suppose I do have a bit of a routine, if you want to call it that. I hope it doesn't sound too strange. \n\nWhen I'm getting ready to post one of those recorded lessons, or before I have to join a virtual parent-teacher conference... I just want to make sure I'm presenting myself well, you know? So I... I might spend a little extra time adjusting the camera angle. Just trying to find a side that doesn't look quite so... off. And I'll replay the clips a few times, just to check. Sometimes I'll catch myself resting my hand on my face again, just to sort of block that side from the camera. \n\nAnd... if I'm being completely honest, and I promise I'm working on getting better at this... sometimes I end up re-recording the whole lesson. A few times, actually. It's just... I want it to be perfect for the kids. But I guess I might do it so much that... well, sometimes I miss my other prep work, or I end up running a little late getting things posted for my classes. \n\nI know it's not ideal, and I really don't want you to think I'm neglecting my students. I care about them so much. I just get so caught up in trying to fix how I look on the screen. Is that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm not coming across as too obsessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concern about your facial appearance has caused you significant distress or has it affected your ability to function normally in your work or personal life?\n\nPatient: I mean... I try so hard not to let it affect my work. I really, really do. I care about my students and the school so much, and I want to be a good teacher for them. I'm really trying my best to fix this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts—like checking your appearance, adjusting your posture, or going over how you look in your mind—because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: I... um, I think we might have touched on this just a little bit earlier, but... yes, I suppose I do. I really try not to make a big deal out of it, and I hope it doesn't sound like I'm completely obsessed or anything. I just want to put my best foot forward for the school and the parents.\n\nBut, well, before I post a recorded lesson or join a virtual parent conference, I do find myself... repeating a few things. Like, I'll keep changing the camera angle, trying to find a spot where my face doesn't look so... uneven. And I'll replay the clips over and over, just to check. I also catch myself resting my hand on my face a lot, just to sort of hide that side. \n\nAnd, like I mentioned before, sometimes I end up re-recording the whole lesson. A few times, actually. It's just... I keep thinking if I try one more time, maybe it won't look so bad. I really try to remind myself of that. But... when I look at those lesson videos, it just seems so glaringly obvious to me. \n\nI just... I can't stop seeing it. I'm trying to be better about leaving work at work, so to speak. I mean, I might still avoid looking in the rearview mirror too long, or I might sit a certain way if I'm talking to my wife, just to... you know, present my better side. \n\nBut it's not like I'm constantly stressing over it every single second of the day. I really try to just live my life and be normal about it. I don't want it to seem like I'm obsessed, because I'm really not. I just... ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... well, I wouldn't say it's completely ruining everything, or at least I really try not to let it. I always want to do a good job and be reliable.

I really am trying my best to get back on track.

It's like... it's just my face, so it's always there. \n\nI try really hard to just ignore it and not make a fuss, especially around my wife. I don't want to bring her down with my... whatever this is. I just tell myself it's not a big deal and try to move on. But, um, I guess I'm not doing a very good job of that if I'm sitting here talking to you about it, right? I'm sorry, I hope I'm not making it sound worse than it is. Does that... does that answer what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: When you notice the crookedness or swelling on one side of your face, do you feel a strong urge to do something—like adjusting your posture, covering your face, or mentally going over how you look—to try to fix or neutralize it?\n\nPatient: I... well, I do try my best not to let it get to me. I really want to be professional and just do my job. But... yes, I suppose I do have a bit of a routine, if you want to call it that. I hope it doesn't sound too strange. \n\nWhen I'm getting ready to post one of those recorded lessons, or before I have to join a virtual parent-teacher conference... I just want to make sure I'm presenting myself well, you know? So I... I might spend a little extra time adjusting the camera angle. Just trying to find a side that doesn't look quite so... off. And I'll replay the clips a few times, just to check. Sometimes I'll catch myself resting my hand on my face again, just to sort of block that side from the camera. \n\nAnd... if I'm being completely honest, and I promise I'm working on getting better at this... sometimes I end up re-recording the whole lesson. A few times, actually. It's just... I want it to be perfect for the kids. But I guess I might do it so much that... well, sometimes I miss my other prep work, or I end up running a little late getting things posted for my classes. \n\nI know it's not ideal, and I really don't want you to think I'm neglecting my students. I care about them so much. I just get so caught up in trying to fix how I look on the screen. Is that... is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I hope I'm not coming across as too obsessive.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your concern about your facial appearance has caused you significant distress or has it affected your ability to function normally in your work or personal life?\n\nPatient: I mean... I try so hard not to let it affect my work. I really, really do. I care about my students and the school so much, and I want to be a good teacher for them. But... well, I guess if I'm being completely honest, and I know this probably sounds terrible... it has been getting a bit difficult lately. \n\nI just... I spend so much time worrying about the videos and trying to fix how I look, that sometimes other things sort of... slip through the cracks? Like, I've fallen a little behind on my regular lesson planning. And I help mentor some of the newer teachers, and I coach the basketball team, but lately, I just... I haven't been able to keep up with all of it because my main work just isn't getting finished on time anymore. \n\nIt's actually gotten to the point where... well, the administration has noticed. They've started documenting some missed deadlines and times when I couldn't cover things I was supposed to. I'm just so terrified that I might be close to losing my position. Which would be awful, because I love teaching. I really do. I just... I feel terrible that I'm letting everyone down. Does that make sense? I hope you don't think I'm a bad teacher... I'm really trying my best to fix this.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts—like checking your appearance, adjusting your posture, or going over how you look in your mind—because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: I... um, I think we might have touched on this just a little bit earlier, but... yes, I suppose I do. I really try not to make a big deal out of it, and I hope it doesn't sound like I'm completely obsessed or anything. I just want to put my best foot forward for the school and the parents.\n\nBut, well, before I post a recorded lesson or join a virtual parent conference, I do find myself... repeating a few things. I really try to remind myself of that. But... when I look at those lesson videos, it just seems so glaringly obvious to me. \n\nI just... I can't stop seeing it. This crooked, swollen-looking side of my face. And once I notice it on the screen, I just get this overwhelming feeling that... well, that everyone else must be seeing it, too. I really do. \n\nBut, I suppose... even when I'm just sitting at my desk or making dinner, it kind of lingers in the back of my mind.
